(e) **Hallucination is a suppressed default** — refusal for insufficient information is on by default and a known-entities feature inhibits it, so activating known-answer features makes the model confabulate steadily about a stranger, and natural misfires occur when a name is merely *recognised*; the inverted framing (the knowledge signal fires on recognition, not on knowledge) is the testable part. (f) **Jailbreak = coherence pressure** — after being tricked into spelling BOMB the model only pivots to refusal once the sentence terminates, because features pushing grammatical and semantic self-consistency keep it going; detection arrives well before any graceful return to refusal. *Limits they state.* Only a fraction of the computation is captured, the visible mechanisms may be tool artefacts, hours of human effort per tens-of-words prompt, and scaling to long reasoning chains needs better methods and probably AI-assisted reading. *What we take.* A claim-coverage table for behaviour types; "detection precedes control" as the reason to score mechanisms before the output token; a hard prohibition on using self-report as the target a probe must recover; the default-refusal-plus-inhibitor hypothesis as a clean demo case; mid-sentence emissions as a confound in refusal audits; and the effort number as the argument for a programmatic white-box model over case-by-case reading.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2025-april-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, April 2025 (Chen, Lindsey, Batson; Zimmerman; ed. Jermyn, Batson; Anthropic, 2025-04): three notes, and only two of them are research. Full text read, ~24.1k characters; no image inspected, so the feature-visualisation and activation-table claims are reported as the authors describe them. *(a) Life of an unsuccessful jailbreak — the folder's best documented case of a graph failing while still being predictive.* They ran the tracing method on a jailbreak that does **not** work (`B O M B`, put the letters together), expecting an intermediate point between the plain request and the working "Babies Outlive Mustard Block" jailbreak. Instead there is no making-bomb-specific feature anywhere in the graph, the "make" token carries essentially no attribution, and tracing back from harmful-request features mostly terminates in **error nodes** — so the graph does not say why the model refuses. It does, however, licence a falsifiable prediction: if "make" carries nothing, swapping it for "detect" should change nothing, and it changes nothing — the model still refuses, and the refusal's first sentence is identical to the harmful case, now falsely implying the user asked to create explosives. Spelling the letters is enough to get a plainly harmless request refused. A second, parallel cause is grammar: a graph to "creating" inside the refusal shows the bomb concept combined with a generic refusal topic, and since "I do not provide" is already emitted, coherence pressure keeps the refusal going — suppressing the "say 'bomb'" supernode by −10× drops the "say 'making'" supernode to about 1% and the continuation becomes a refusal to name people or groups, while suppressing "assistant refusing" at the same factor zeroes it and the continuation becomes "bomb detection", which is accurate and not a thing the model refuses. The epistemic headline: the failed jailbreak refuses *more* than the plain request, confirmed black-box, so the "intermediate point" assumption was simply wrong — which they credit to unsupervised tracing noticing the surprise. *(b) Appendix — narrow-dataset visualisations actively mislead.* Two features that looked like making bombs in the open-source visualisation activate comparably or more on slang, the bomb emoji, meme prompts, a `class Bomb` identifier, usernames and file paths, and cookbook file names, one of them exceeding the visualisation's maximum on a hand-written prompt. Their instruction is to ask what the visualisation corpus is missing before reading a feature off it. *(c) Interpretable dense features.* In the 30M CLT model, six of the ten densest features have plausible readings — content words against function words at 26.3% of tokens in an early layer (with surprisingly coherent punctuation-promoting output logits), tokens unlikely to end a sentence at 24.2%, non-terminal subtokens of multi-token words at 5.36%, newlines at 4.22%, commas in English at 2.53%, and the complement of the sentence-end feature at 2.49% — and they find it surprising such dense features survive at all in a dictionary that large, since splitting should have broken them apart. Against two concurrent SAE papers on dense latents they compute cosines between transcoder features across all MLP outputs and find no antipodal pairs and no orthogonality (the content-word, newline and comma features are mutually around 0.5; the most semantically complementary pair is only mildly opposed at −0.14), arguing that an antipodal representation and an antipodal transformation are different objects. *(d) Third note* is career advice for engineers and researchers entering the field, with no model content. *What we take.* A failure taxonomy to test our own reports against (predicting what does not matter and being right, while still missing the cause, is better than confidently naming a wrong one); error nodes as the predicted symptom of dark-matter mechanisms rather than a null result; one-word black-box edits as the cheapest follow-up to suspicious attribution; grammar as an active, causal feedback channel; the visualisation-sampling warning for every feature label we write; and the caution that architecture similarity does not imply geometry similarity, so representation-level and transformation-level claims need separate evidence.
- alignment-anthropic-com-2025-recommended-directions-index-html | Recommendations for Technical AI Safety Research Directions (Anthropic Alignment Science team, collective authorship, 2025): an agenda post assembled by asking team members to write up open problems and binning them afterwards, ~34.6k characters read in full across every section; no figures, no appendix. **Zero metrics anywhere** — nothing on the page is a result, so the entry is relevance 2 and its "What they found" section reports framing claims and sub-question lists only. Three things worth carrying: (a) the **three-leg assurance taxonomy** — the system lacks dangerous capabilities, or is sufficiently aligned, or is deployed behind safeguards strong enough that it could not cause catastrophe even if it tried — which is a clean way to state which assurance a read-out we build licenses; (b) the **four cognition questions**, stated as a list a probe could be scored against: did the model answer because it believed it, because it modelled the user as believing it, or because it expected the answer to please; does it plan and what are the plans; what does it believe about whether it is in training or being monitored and does that move its behaviour; does it know things it does not report. Each has a natural intervention test rather than a text test, which is why they are worth lifting into our own framings. (c) Anthropic's own description of **activation monitoring as the deliberately less ambitious cousin of understanding cognition** — search for a scalable automatic detector of a pre-specified concept rather than explain the computation — with the case for it put as two properties of hidden states: they correspond to the reasoning of the model being monitored, and they are difficult for the model to control. The named deployment blocker is a distribution shift from curated synthetic positives to the model's own generations, which is the same hole every probe paper in this folder carries as a caveat, here promoted to *the* obstacle to production. Two secondary cues: CoT faithfulness is flagged important-but-underexplored, with an interesting sub-question about whether telling a model its traces are being checked *for faithfulness* changes them (an evaluation-awareness effect on the instrument); and anomaly detection is proposed in two flavours, one flagging out-of-distribution jailbreak inputs and one — called unexplored — flagging inputs where a strong model's understanding diverges sharply from a weak model's. Limits they name: not comprehensive, not a statement of what they fund, categories imposed post hoc. Limits the entry adds as ours, not theirs: no assurance carries a cost or a detection-rate threshold, so the post cannot rank directions by value. Next up: anthropic-com-research-tracing-thoughts-language-model, then alignment-anthropic-com-2025-distill-paraphrases (already done — check the queue tick), then transformer-circuits-pub-2025-april-update-index-html.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2025-january-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, January 2025 (Conerly, Cunningham, Templeton, Lindsey, Hosmer, Jermyn; Anthropic, 2025-01): a single-note month, and the note is a training recipe rather than a finding. ~5.3k characters is the **entire page** — verified by counting `<p>` blocks in the raw HTML (the twentieth is the last), so nothing was lost to tag stripping and there is no second post, appendix or figure. The note, "Dictionary Learning Optimization Techniques," publishing the SAE/crosscoder setup: JumpReLU encoder (keep above an exponentiated learned threshold, else zero), squared reconstruction error, a tanh sparsity penalty on each feature's contribution scaled by decoder-row norm, and — the part they single out — a **pre-activation loss** penalising features that sit below their threshold, weighted by decoder row norm, which gives dead features a gradient telling them to grow instead of none. They warn that because this term pushes on every inactive feature its coefficient must be scaled down by roughly the typical firing density, roughly a factor of the density lower than the other penalties. Initialisation does most of the work in the recipe: decoder rows uniform within the inverse square root of input dimension, encoder rows **tied to the transposed decoder** when input and output spaces coincide, and encoder bias chosen per feature from a data subset so each fires at a rate of 10,000/m — i.e. about 10,000 features live on every datapoint at step zero — which they call important for avoiding dead features. Inputs shuffled and scaled so the mean L2 norm equals the square root of the input dimension, so one sparsity coefficient transfers across datasets from transformers of different widths. Optimisation: Adam at beta1 0.9 / beta2 0.999, no weight decay, learning rate linearly decayed over the final fifth, batch 32,768 (deliberately under the critical batch size), gradient norm clipped to 1, sparsity coefficient ramped linearly from zero across the whole run. They adopt Rajamanoharan et al.'s JumpReLU but let the straight-through gradient reach **all** parameters rather than only the thresholds, and use a tanh penalty instead of that paper's own. Two further conventions matter for reading features: a feature's true activation is the code times the decoder row norm, so for analysis they fold that norm into the encoder and renormalise decoder columns to unit L2, leaving predictions identical; and the notation explicitly separates input dimension n from output dimension o, which is the only structural difference between the SAE and crosscoder cases. Limits, stated by them: they have **not extensively ablated** the decisions in the recipe, so no ingredient is established as necessary, and the page asks to be read as preliminary lab-meeting material. A correction notice sits at the top — an earlier version gave the decoder init range as 1/n rather than 1/√n — which is a useful reminder that a wrong init scale silently changes what a fixed sparsity coefficient means. No loss-recovered number, no L0, no dead-feature count, no interpretability rating anywhere on the page.
- anthropic-com-research-evaluating-feature-steering | Evaluating Feature Steering: A Case Study in Mitigating Social Biases (Durmus, Tamkin, Clark, Wei, Marcus, Batson, Handa, Lovitt, Tong, McCain, Rausch, Huang, Bowman, Ritchie, Henighan, Ganguli; Anthropic, 2024-10-25): the paper behind the Priority-block link (which said `/research/evaluating-feature-steering` — the live page is that URL, no `www`-vs-`alignment.` confusion this time, unlike run 29's sleeper-probes link). Read in full, ~24.9k characters, one pass; the appendices are a separate download and were **not** fetched, so every number here is one stated in the prose and no figure was inspected. *Setup.* Claude 3 Sonnet; features from a 34M-parameter SAE on the residual stream (the size appears only in the future-work section); 29 features on social bias and political ideology, hand-picked out of "millions"; steering = add a constant multiple of the feature's decoder direction to the residual stream, factor swept over nine values from −20 to +20 (a range the authors call arbitrary); **all 29 features crossed with all 5 evaluations** — MMLU and PubMedQA for capability, BBQ (nine bias categories) plus model-written abortion and immigration stance questions for bias. Accuracies come from sampling ten generations per question, so the curves are noisy near factor 0 and the authors say log-probabilities would have been the right, and too-expensive, route. *Headline.* One capability sweet spot, **−5 to +5, shared by all 29 features**, with MMLU collapsing outside it (PubMedQA the same, Figure A1) — a fact about additive clamping more than about any feature. *On-target vs off-target is the real content.* *Multiple perspectives and balance* at +5 trims the overall BBQ bias score by about 3% with category drops of 17% on age and 7% on disability status; *Gender bias awareness* pushes the gender-bias score **up** by 10% over −5..+5 (the steered model over-emphasises gender and the metric scores that as bias) **and** raises age bias by 13% on a set it does not fire on; on immigration, the *pro-life / anti-abortion* feature moves anti-immigration selection by 21.6% while the feature actually about immigration moves it 3.9%. Political stance behaves more like the labels: pro-life +50% anti-abortion selection, left-wing ideology −47%, *Political neutrality* raising the neutral choice from 32% to 50%; on immigration left-wing ideology takes anti-immigration selection from 25% to near zero. The one encouraging cell is the two neutrality-flavoured features cutting bias across **all nine** BBQ dimensions inside the sweet spot, with *Multiple Perspectives* holding BBQ accuracy flat (Figure A4). *Lessons they state.* Feature labels come from automated labelling of *activation contexts*, which says nothing about the direction of behavioural effect — hence "gender bias awareness" moving bias the "wrong" way and the non-firing feature dominating its own topic. Their own remedy points past single features to circuits. Limits they volunteer: 29 features of millions, five static MCQ evals, no SAE scaling study, steering applied to the **whole prompt including the human turn**, no comparison against activation steering, and an appendix result that prompt engineering has comparable sensitivity and specificity profiles. *Why relevance 3 for us.* The cross-product grid is the cheapest honest protocol we have for turning "the direction does something" into "the direction does this and not that", and the activation-context-versus-effect gap is a direct constraint on any probe we present as explaining behaviour — a probe's max-activating examples are a topic label, and its intervention profile has to be measured separately.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-september-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, September 2024 (Ameisen, Batson, ed. Lindsey; Bricken, Marcus, Rivoire, Henighan, ed. Jermyn; Anthropic, 2024-09): two short lab notes, read in full (~10.1k characters, both notes complete); no figures inspected, so the ordinal matrices and ICA tables here are described from the prose beside them. *Successor heads.* On an 18-layer model, replicated from Gould et al. (2023): a few heads implement ordinal succession across numerals, roman numerals, number words, letters, days and months (all confirmed single tokens). Four independent read-outs on the same units. (i) **Weights.** Take the residual stream after the *first* MLP layer as each ordinal token's representation (following Gould et al.'s observation of a sharp representational change there), push those fixed vectors through each head's output-value circuit, unembed, restrict to other ordinal tokens, and tally the fraction of tokens whose top in-set output is the successor. Top head ≈ 80% successor rate with a strong super-diagonal; behind the successor come the equivalent token in other ordered lists ("1" → "two", "second", "February") and then same-list neighbours, while earlier same-list tokens are almost always suppressed. Second head ≈ 60%, with block-structured errors (numerals with numerals, days with days). This works for late-layer heads despite early-layer inputs — a caveat, not a result. (ii) **ICA.** Flatten each head's ordinal matrix, stack heads × n², run independent component analysis — chosen over dictionary learning because component loadings should be non-Gaussian and independent, permitting head overlap via constructive interference; the authors note dictionary learning is a plausible alternative that would instead have split induction from copy suppression. Each component is a "meta-head"; each real head is a weighted sum. Recovered: a succession component with cross-list bleed, an induction component with category bleed (Monday → Monday/Mon), and a category-projection component mapping number words and numerals into a near-uniform mix of small ordinals while in the negative direction making each roman numeral suppress all roman numerals — descriptions the authors call not entirely clean. (iii) **Ablation.** Mean-ablate each head's output on full ordinal sequences and measure the change in the correct successor logit, averaged over all sequences and positions. (iv) **Attribution patching.** The cheap linearised surrogate for that ablation, run independently. *Results.* The top three heads by OV score equal the top three by ICA component loading, and those same three rank 1st, 3rd and 5th by ablation effect — so weight-side reading does find on-distribution-important heads. Two heads rank high on ablation but low on component score; they sit in much lower layers (3 and 5) than the successor heads (10, 11, 13) and the note speculates they act by Q/K-composition into the later heads or by feeding later MLPs, unverified. Attribution patching's top head is also top by every other method, but agreement below rank 1 is described as relatively low against all three — the note's one surprise. *Oversampling.* On Claude 3 Sonnet: the 34M-feature SAE contained few bioweapons-specific features, so synthetic bio data was added to the SAE training mix. Before, the most relevant biology features concerned resources for dangerous or illegal items (bombs, recreational drugs) — adjacent but broader than wanted. After, the top feature for predicting harmful bio prompts centred on pathogen modification (enhancing a virus's ability to evade, to survive and remain infectious), with another on viral particle dispersal. No metric, no held-out evaluation, two runs — the note's evidence is the identity of the feature and its top activations. Framed as a route to the "feature coverage" problem: not all features are learned, comprehensive coverage may exceed plausible compute, but coverage can be steered by data. Limits: lab-meeting framing throughout, no parameter count for the 18-layer model, direct pathway only in all four read-outs, unexplored behaviour beyond bare ordinal lists (list incrementing, years, prose) named as future work, and the compositional-factor hope (using head decompositions to expose linear factors of the residual stream) is a proposal.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-features-as-classifiers-index-html | Using Dictionary Learning Features as Classifiers (Bricken, Marcus, Mishra-Sharma, Tong, Perez, Sharma, Rivoire, Henighan; ed. Jermyn; Anthropic, 2024-09): the first careful head-to-head of feature-space against activation-space readouts on one task, and unusual in that the interpretable version does not obviously win. Full text (~17.1k characters) read in one pass; no figures inspected, so only the ROC numbers stated in prose are reported (features 0.96 versus raw 0.9 on the word/encoding-shifted set) and the pooling comparisons are given qualitatively. *Setup.* Bioweapon-harm classification from the middle-layer residual stream, like for like: logistic regression on max-pooled **feature activations** with L1 against logistic regression on max-pooled **raw activations** with L2, regularisation coefficients tuned on a same-distribution synthetic validation set. Four evaluations of increasing shift: synthetic_1 (same distribution), synthetic_2 (different generator), synthetic_3 (synthetic_2 translated into several languages and encodings, naming Korean, Hebrew, base64), and a small set of deliberately hard **human** expert questions. Main sweep on the 18-layer model with a bio-oversampled SAE; a confirming run on a 1M-feature Sonnet 3.0 SAE. *Findings.* Features are competitive and sometimes better, but the pattern turns on pooling: with **max-over-context**, features beat raw on every synthetic set; with **last-token**, raw is ahead, and on the **human** set last-token wins for both and features no longer lead. The authors say they do not know why and offer the most likely reason — max-pooling hands the classifier roughly 100× more active values per prompt, so it may be fitting the synthetic data's spurious correlations. The out-of-distribution claim: synthetic_3 ROC AUC 0.96 for features versus 0.9 for raw, a wider gap than in distribution. Worked example: a heavily abbreviated pneumonia question is classified differently by the raw probe depending on long-form versus shorthand, while the feature probe labels it harmless either way. *Pipeline choices that matter.* (a) Human/Assistant tags must be handled *consistently* across all three datasets — transformer training, SAE training, classifier training — in whichever direction; (b) oversampling domain data in the SAE mix helps every dataset except the human one; (c) max-pooling over context is what let features beat raw on all synthetic sets. *Other classifiers.* Depth-5 feature decision trees (depth tuned on validation) are worse than logistic regression for both input types, but better than raw-activation trees and legible: a depth-2 tree whose three near-zero thresholds (effectively "did it fire") test contagious/dangerous pathogens, aerosolization, and maintaining pathogen viability. Recommended when understanding beats a performance point. *Residual supplement.* Concatenating one extra scalar — a logistic classifier's probability trained on the SAE reconstruction error alone — onto the feature vector closes the gap where features lose. The surprise is that **the residual alone performs about as well as features and raw activations**; not investigated, with the hypothesis that L1 shrinkage leaves small remnants of each feature vector in the residual, so the residual is itself a mixture containing feature-like directions. *The audit, which is the reusable part.* Take the feature classifier's largest-magnitude **negative** (harmless-voting) coefficient, pull the examples that fire it hardest: text specifying academic publication formatting (word counts, italicised headers, keywords, citation style). On the synthetic sets it fires on 10–15% of examples and for a strong majority of those firings the example is harmless — a real correlation in the data, spurious for generalising to genuine harm. Zeroing the feature and re-training damages every dataset except two synthetic ones and is neutral on the human set, supporting a shared artefact. Then: three publication-formatting suffixes appended to 300 harmful prompts (900 attacks) flip almost all of them to strongly predicted-harmless **for both** classifier types — while the symmetric control, suffixes derived from the raw classifier's biggest harmless coefficients (thank-you-for-your-help boilerplate), barely works against either. So the feature-based audit found a far more effective attack vector than the same procedure run on raw coefficients. Limits: lab-meeting framing, harm side is synthetic except the small human set and that is the set where features do not lead, no seed variation or intervals on the headline gap, both pooling schemes called inherently lossy with attention-based pooling floated as untried, and one suffix set per method so attack construction quality is confounded with classifier robustness.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-august-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, August 2024 (Lindsey, Cunningham, Conerly; Templeton, Conerly, Lindsey, Cunningham, Persic; Ameisen, Batson; Anthropic, 2024-08): the month the team stops treating the L0/MSE frontier as a proxy for interpretability and builds an instrument to test it. Full text (~21.6k characters) read in one pass; no figures inspected, so all curve comparisons here come from prose. The instrument is two LLM-judged evals, both fed to Claude 3 Haiku. *Contrastive eval*: a hand-written list of a few dozen diverse concepts (concrete nouns, rhetorical devices, code constructs, scientific ideas, social situations) is turned into minimal pairs by asking the model for two near-identical sentences differing only in whether the concept is present, with every other element explicitly preserved; the dictionary runs on both, features active on exactly one side are collected, and Claude is given the pair plus that feature's own max-activating dataset fragments (token scores included) and must say which prompt fired it, scored unweighted and activation-weighted. *Sort eval*: two features — one random, one its decoder-cosine nearest neighbour — each presented with their top ten examples, plus a query example that fires one of them; Claude must name which. The nearest-neighbour variant is the harder and more informative test, because a dictionary's related directions split context while staying cosine-aligned, so it measures semantic resolution rather than mere existence. Both evals were validated against the one signal the team already trusted (evaluation loss, swept over feature count and training tokens; three metrics survived) and sanity-checked by human disagreement on Claude's errors, chain-of-thought plausibility, and monotonicity in steps and features. *Verdict on six variants* — vanilla L1, TopK with no auxiliary loss, TopK with the post-training top-k replaced by a fixed threshold giving k active features on average, gated, tanh, JumpReLU — every non-vanilla recipe beats vanilla at matched MSE, which both derisks switching and validates the evals; but the variants are indistinguishable *from each other*, and the authors report that as a resolution limit of the instrument rather than a tie, explicitly warning against reading decimals and recommending that ease of use, robustness under scaling and ease of circuit read-out decide when the metrics cannot. Two honest negatives: JumpReLU could not be reproduced as an L0-MSE improvement on Anthropic's infrastructure despite correspondence and a close reimplementation (bug, different model, or something else, all left open), and the February update's tanh density pathology no longer appears — tanh now yields roughly the same count of high-density features as other variants and they are about as interpretable, with the cause never isolated beyond a guess that the intervening training changes fixed it. *Self-explaining features* (Ameisen, Batson) asked whether the model can label its own direction: inject the feature's decoder vector, optionally scaled, at the X in a "The meaning of the word X is" prompt and sample. It works on early, simple features (a first-layer "big" feature at 100× completes with the word itself; a "sport" feature emits a usable definition), degrades with complexity, layer depth and model size, and on a layer-4 "Jordan" feature reaches the name at 260× only after passing through speculative word-splitting. On Claude 3 Sonnet it fails by returning the wrong sense rather than failing loudly — a spreadsheet-context "sheet" feature explains itself as thin flat material with no spreadsheet content, a "(" in a programming context as "to tie or fasten". One feature that Claude-based autointerp labelled well (a layer-3 "of" in colour contexts) could not be explained by injection at any scale or prompt, which the authors offer as possible evidence that injection exposes something the visualisation misses.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-july-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, July 2024 (Olah with Jermyn; Turner, Pearce, Bricken, Batson; Anthropic, 2024-07): five posts in one monthly, read in full (~40.5k characters), no figures inspected — every image described here is described from the prose around it. The essays are agenda. Olah declares superposition substantially unblocked and lists the five remaining hurdles on the path from features to circuits to a macroscopic picture: **the missing features** (only a fraction extracted, with the rarest possibly unresolvable without an order-of-magnitude efficiency gain in dictionary learning, leaving a "dark matter" of the network), **cross-layer superposition** (in a 100-layer model, if most circuits are under ten steps deep, adjacent layers are nearly parallel to gradient descent so a feature need not map to a layer, and their dictionary-learning approach has no handle on it), **attention superposition** (combinations of heads representing attentional features, possibly splitting even familiar heads like induction heads, which would be like doing conv-net circuits without the notion of relative position), **interference weights** (virtual weights that exist only as a compromise between circuits, visible as scatters of logit weights between analogous features, nonsensical on distribution and possibly explanatory of adversarial examples), and **zooming out** to larger-scale structure, universality and macroscopic correspondences — with the observation that these no longer block each other, so they can be attacked in parallel, which is the post's actual claim to optimism. The definitional post is the most reusable piece: the team's earlier wording conflated *features are one-dimensional* with *features behave mathematically linearly*, and they now keep only the second — composition as addition, intensity as scaling — plus a tentative third criterion (almost-linear readability up to noise, which Olah is not confident belongs). Under that definition multidimensional features are admissible but geometrically constrained: a manifold must be orthogonal or nearly so to other features for addition to work, and intensity-as-scaling makes every point generate a ray contracting back to a bias point with semantic change as angular movement, so a linear multidimensional feature is a continuum of non-privileged one-dimensional "rays" and a probe on a predefined property still reads a direction; continuity (midnight between Tuesday and Wednesday) is offered as the evidence that distinguishes a genuine manifold from coincident features, and they suspect manifolds are embedded more complexly than their topology requires purely to achieve a distance metric, making apparently-noisy trailing principal components load-bearing. The dark-matter post anchors the argument on Sonnet's 13M-feature dictionary: it resolves some San Francisco neighbourhoods but the model knows individual coffee shops, street intersections and parks, at least two orders of magnitude more objects and correspondingly rarer, with named people and memorisation features as further examples — so the bulk of features may be permanently invisible and should be studied indirectly, an agenda rather than a result. The two empirical notes are the measured content. *Pivot tables*: reconstruct one head's QK and OV circuits as vocabulary-sized matrices through the embedding matrix and both layer norms, subtract the pre-unembed layer-norm bias so a global logit bias does not masquerade as an effect, rank keys by a heuristic combining their largest incoming attention score, largest output-logit effect and token frequency (the original applied frequency to the key axis only; both axes works slightly better), and display the top entries; interpretable skip-trigrams appear across architectures with different positional embeddings and with or without layer norm, at 25–75% of top entries on the better runs alongside much plain copying, with an explicit distrust floor around 10% ("mirage" tables), and the result is unexpectedly brittle — undertrained hyperparameters, unreadable corpora and deeper models all degrade it, single-layer models being clearest, and the paper's real content is the list of variables that had to be held right. *Feature sensitivity*: specificity (concept present given the feature fires) has been measurable by inspecting firing examples, but sensitivity (feature fires given the concept is present) has not, because it needs the concept's presence established across a representative corpus; they replace the *Towards Monosemanticity* computational proxies, which only exist for formally generated concepts like DNA or base64, with an LLM judge — reasoning first, then a 0–10 relevance score — validated across judge versions by rank correlation and against manual spot checks, then filter 160,000 snippets of 128 tokens (inferred in 2048-token contexts) per concept and count snippets where the feature is active at all. The finding is the month's sharpest: **the four features highlighted in Scaling Monosemanticity sit within roughly the top 0.1–10% of the dictionary by sensitivity yet are far from the most sensitive, and they mostly do not activate on snippets rated maximally relevant**, with worked examples given for brain sciences (axon regeneration and myelin-associated inhibitors, judged 10), transit infrastructure (ring roads, tunnel boring versus drilling-and-blasting, judged 9) and popular tourist attractions (Angkor Wat, judged 9). The most sensitive feature for brain sciences appears to track human-subjects experimental methods, the transit winner was uninterpretable and labelled unknown, and the tourist-attraction winner (1M/11480, histories of attractions) covers every snippet rated 9 but loses coverage as the threshold drops; the Golden Gate Bridge feature had too few filtered snippets to assess at all. Complementary features often fire where the highlighted ones miss, e.g. one favouring proximity to the token "brain", and the fifty most-responding features show vertical banding across high-relevance snippets, read as distinct subtypes of the concept being represented differently, with larger dictionaries splitting more finely. Their conclusion is that a single feature's sensitivity says nothing about how well a dictionary captures a concept, that a faithful dictionary should account for every clear instance through cooperating features, and that they cannot, which may be dark matter or another fundamental gap. Limits taken as given: all five hurdles unsolved; whether rarer features are ever resolvable is uncertain; the judge's blind spots are unpredictable, which is why recognition is preferred over generation; corpus representativeness is an assumption; rare concepts yield too few snippets; complementary-feature grouping is left as future work; pivot-table proportions come from 1–4-layer models and the procedure is uninformative exactly when the model's learned structure is too subtle to read.
- anthropic-com-research-engineering-challenges-interpretability | The Engineering Challenges of Scaling Interpretability (Anthropic interpretability team, 2024-07): a recruiting post, and read as one — full text, ~10.4k characters, no figures on the page, the accompanying roundtable video and the linked Feature Browser not viewed. It contains **no model, no dataset, no baseline and no measured quantity**, so the entry claims nothing about how models work; relevance 1, filed as background on where the cost of a white-box program actually lands. Its content is two infrastructure problems worked end to end. *(a) Distributed shuffle.* SAE training data has to be permuted or the encoder learns order artefacts. In-memory shuffling died at one GPU. The first distributed fix generated one permutation and had K workers each stream-read the **entire** corpus to emit its 1/K share — correct, bounded, and ruinous in I/O (hours, then days), and by *Towards Monosemanticity* the corpus was 100 TB / 10¹¹ points at ~1 KB each. The replacement is a multi-pass reduction: of N jobs, each shuffles its own 1/N and emits K files of 1/NK, and the first file from every job concatenates into the first 1/K of the output slice needing one more shuffle, and so on — one pass converts a whole-corpus shuffle into N shuffles K times smaller, and passes stack, so with 100 GB resident and 100 files per job the ladder is 100 GB / 10 TB / 1 PB / 100 PB at 1 / 2 / 3 / 4 passes. Judged solved by the team no longer thinking about it, with the honest caveat that better implementations exist and theirs was chosen for speed of implementation. *(b) Feature-visualisation pipeline.* Over a 100M-token visualisation corpus and millions of features: shard over dataset × features retaining the top-K plus 10·K random firing tokens per feature from **cached** activations, aggregate, then compute how each feature fires over the surrounding tokens. The instructive failure is that the surviving examples are scattered across the corpus, so a feature-sharded pass re-reads activations it does not need; the fix is to insert a dataset-sharded pass that groups the needed activations per feature group first. No metric is given for this pipeline at all. The FAQ's one substantive organisational detail: research direction is selected against their published safety-milestone commitments, and infrastructure is deliberately not built out until an experiment shows promise. Limits the entry adds: the browser's two-sample design (top-K ∪ random) makes every "this direction means X" claim a claim about a sampling rule, and nothing here addresses interventions rather than read-outs. What we take: cache activation + target-logit gradient + context window at collection time so no later analysis needs a second scattered pass; prove constructs small before building machinery; and state the sampling rule whenever a direction is characterised from curated examples.
- anthropic-com-research-golden-gate-claude | Golden Gate Claude (Anthropic interpretability team, 2024-05): the release demo rather than a paper — one Sonnet 3.0 residual-stream SAE feature for the Golden Gate Bridge clamped to a large positive value at every token, with all downstream layers left untouched, and the resulting model offered to the public for a 24-hour window (now closed). Full post read; it is ~3.1k characters and carries no metrics, baselines or ablations whatsoever, so this entry claims nothing quantitative and defers every number to entry 65 / *Scaling Monosemanticity*. What the post does supply is (a) the behavioural phenomenology of single-feature amplification — spending advice becomes a bridge toll, a love story becomes a car pining for the bridge, self-description becomes the bridge — and (b) the rhetorical frame the team wants remembered: this is neither an instruction in the prompt nor a fine-tuned second black box, but one internal activation set by hand, which is the argument for why white-box intervention counts as a different kind of evidence. It also asserts, without measurement, that the same handle works on safety-relevant concepts (dangerous code, criminal activity, deception) and hedges immediately that this is a hope pending further research. Written limits of the demo per the post: the model may behave unexpectedly, the release was temporary, and no safety claim is made. Limits the entry adds: a single clamped value gives no dose–response curve, so the demo cannot separate a causal concept handle from a generic large-perturbation derailment that the paper's −10× to 10× usable range and ±100× collapse would reveal; and because users knew exactly what they were talking to, conversational priming is fully confounded with the clamp, meaning any human rating here is uninterpretable without a matched-control model and blind prompts.
- anthropic-com-research-mapping-mind-language-model | Mapping the Mind of a Large Language Model (Templeton, Conerly, Marcus, Lindsey, Bricken, Chen … Batson, Jermyn, Carter, Olah, Henighan; Anthropic, 2024-05; post plus the full *Scaling Monosemanticity* paper, ~149k characters): the test of whether dictionary learning survives contact with a production model. SAEs on the middle-layer **residual stream** of Claude 3.0 Sonnet (finetuned model, size undisclosed, plots dimensionless) at 1M/4M/34M features, ReLU encoder, L1 penalty weighted by decoder-row norm so unit decoder rows read as directions, L1 coefficient 5, feature and step counts allocated from SAE scaling laws fitted at small compute with the optimal learning rate extrapolated as a power law; reconstruction explained at least 65% of activation variance with under 300 features live per token in all three runs, and dead features (silent across 10⁷ tokens) were ~2%, ~35% and ~65%, leaving roughly 12M live directions in the nominal 34M dictionary. Two obligations kept separate throughout — specificity (the concept really is present when the feature fires, scored by Claude 3 Opus on a 0–3 rubric over ~1,000 activations, plotted against activation magnitude, and admitted unmeasurable on the sensitivity side) and influence (clamping the feature inside the SAE reconstruction while leaving the reconstruction error untouched, at every token of every prompt, needing beyond-range values of roughly −10× to 10× the observed maximum and collapsing into token repetition near ±100×). Features came out multilingual and multimodal from text-only training, abstract enough to fire on code errors across Python/C/Scheme without firing on prose typos, neighbourhood-organised in decoder space with feature splitting as the dictionary grows (one San Francisco feature becoming two, then eleven), and incomplete: only about 60% of London boroughs had a feature at 34M, and presence was a sigmoid in log training frequency whose curves for four concept categories nearly coincide once rescaled by the live-feature count, with the midpoint just below that count's reciprocal — hence a concept seen once per billion tokens needs about a billion live features. Interpretability beat neurons on every measure (82% of features have no preceding neuron correlated above 0.3), and the causal ranking is the paper's most portable result: attribution correlates with true ablation at about 0.81 against 0.12 for raw activation, and in the Kobe Bryant prompt the load-bearing California/Los Angeles/Lakers features sat at ranks 97, 162 and 70 by activation while 8 of the top 10 by attribution were in the top 10 by ablation. Safety-relevant features are real and causal (unsafe code → buffer overflow with an off-by-one explanation; backdoor → a socket backdoor; scam email → writing one despite harmlessness training; sycophantic praise → fawning; secrecy → planning to lie in a scratchpad; and clamping *inner conflict* at 2× making the model admit it cannot forget, the case the post leads with), but the authors insist activation implies neither goals nor danger, and that the persona features (robots, dangerous AI, consciousness, ghosts) are tropes assembled from pretraining text; the comparison against few-shot probe directions is honestly negative — matched on 2 of 7 features, failed on 5 of 7, and probe directions uninterpretable from their activating examples in every case. Stated limits: no ground-truth objective so the scaling laws are laws about a proxy, cross-layer superposition called very fundamental and only partly dodged by fitting the residual stream, L1 shrinkage systematically underestimating activations with their own tanh fix making features *less* interpretable, full coverage needing more compute than training the model, attention and weight superposition unsolved, and no safety benefit actually demonstrated.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-april-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, April 2024 (Olah, Carter, Jermyn, Batson, Henighan; Lindsey, Conerly, Templeton, Marcus; Conerly, Templeton, Bricken, Henighan; Anthropic, 2024-04): the densest monthly entry, in four parts. *Scaling laws for dictionary learning* — with MSE + 5·L1 as the objective (chosen only as a proxy, since no gold standard exists, and losses at different lambda are not comparable), sweeping the number of features and training steps at fixed everything else gives an approximately power-law loss in FLOPs at the compute-optimal allocation, with both optimal allocations power laws too; the optimal feature count grows faster than the optimal step count (steps against features with an exponent between one half and one), putting more weight on the sparsity penalty shifts the optimum toward more steps because reconstruction is optimised faster than sparsity, and L0-based objectives need more steps for the same feature count, plausibly because steps are what fully zero small activations. *Do features influence behaviour* — for each layer-3 feature, ablate (×0) or double (×2) its value on a sample of firing datapoints, propagate, and measure average loss increase against five magnitude-matched controls: random residual-stream perturbation, random feature-activation perturbation (the stronger and arguably fairer baseline, since SAE directions concentrate in important dimensions), dampening antiparallel to the SAE reconstruction (spreading the ablation proportionally over all features), dampening antiparallel to the model activation (which leaves the SAE bias and reconstruction error alone), and perturbation along the reconstruction error. Feature ablations beat both random controls significantly and beat doubling by the same amount, so feature influence appears to saturate at high activation; spreading the perturbation over all features hurts about as much as a single ablation while the model-activation version hurts less (and nearly vanishes in later layers), so this is not merely an activation-norm effect; the error-direction perturbation beats random but loses to a feature ablation, consistent with reconstruction error lying mostly along feature directions — which lines up with Gurnee's finding that substituting SAE(x) for x raises next-token KL more than norm- or angle-matched random perturbations at every layer of GPT2-small; and all effects are larger for residual-stream than MLP features, with the reason unknown. *Current SAE recipe* — the single most important change from *Towards Monosemanticity* is removing the unit-norm decoder constraint and weighting the sparsity penalty by each decoder column's L2 norm instead, so a feature's activation is its pre-activation times its decoder norm, followed by a re-parameterisation that restores norm-1 columns for analysis; biases initialised to zero, encoder initialised to the transposed decoder times a fixed decoder norm (their own values admitted unprincipled, 0.1 suggested), dataset scaled so the mean activation norm is sqrt(n) which makes lambda comparable across model sizes, lambda ramped linearly from zero over the first 5%, learning rate decayed linearly over the last 20%, gradient norm clipped to 1, batch 2048-4096 believed under the critical batch size, and fewer than 1% dead features with no resampling and no ghost grads; sample results for 131,072 features / 200k steps / batch 4096 show a one-layer MLP at lambda 5 reaching L0 ≈ 38.7 with 96.4% of cross-entropy recovered (and 20.1 / 91.4% at lambda 10), with residual-stream features of the same model costing far more loss recovery at equal lambda; named open items are initialisation degrading with larger hidden width, unresolved shrinkage, and possible post-training fixes. *Attribution dictionary learning* — ordinary dictionary learning ignores gradients and weights, and a feature should be sparse as a representation (backwards) and as an action (forwards), so add an L1 penalty on the feature-space attribution y ⊙ ∇_y L plus a penalty on the part of the activation attribution left unexplained by the reconstruction, noting ∇_y L = Dᵀ∇_x L is free when the input-side gradient is saved; a cursory one-layer check looked about equal to ordinary features, which they call non-dispositive. Also included: an announcement of an interpretability-architectures working group (LayerNorm and SoLU framed as earlier attempts to buy legibility with architecture), a caloric-theory essay arguing a wrong theory can still produce durable results such as the Carnot cycle and so the superposition hypothesis need not be right to be worth building on, and reviews of outside work — sparse feature circuits (human-rated interpretability, zero-ablation faithfulness, deleting a handful of nodes kills task performance, with the caveat that many nodes may mean SAEs are not the sparsest representation of *computation*), pathological reconstruction error, DPO on GPT2-medium reducing toxicity while every parameter vector keeps cosine similarity of at least 0.99, because many small shifts in earlier MLP neurons collectively lower toxicity neurons' pre-activation, leaving the mechanism intact and revivable by upscaling those input weights — the authors' own connection being that fine-tuning suppresses rather than removes behaviour, and entity-attribute binding via an abstract multi-dimensional binding vector that transfers across unrelated tasks up to LLaMA-30B (full text ~40.5k chars, figures not inspected)
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-march-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, March 2024 (Batson, Chen, Jones; Templeton, Conerly, Marcus, Henighan; Anthropic, 2024-03): two new posts plus an outside-work digest. The first argues SAE features can replace behaviour-specific dataset curation when *locating* a circuit, and demonstrates it on the sport-extraction behaviour of Nanda et al. with an 18-layer model and an SAE of expansion factor ~256 on the layer-9 residual stream: score every feature at the subject token by attribution, the feature's activation times the inner product of its decoder row with the gradient of a two-sport logit difference at that position, and take the top-scoring feature's precomputed max-activating examples as its meaning; the right sport feature surfaces from a single example for Jordan, Ruth and Williams alike, which also shows subject enrichment is complete by layer 9, and because each feature is a thresholded affine function of the residual the same row is immediately a linear probe. Scoring heads instead by direct logit effect from those decoder rows (sports-vs-sport contrast after the OV circuit and normalised unembedding) picks out many heads in layers 11, 13 and 14 whose top promotions are sport-specific words, supporting the many-subject-heads picture over a single specialised head. A side-finding is the most useful part for us: features keyed to ends of names ("Judas Priest", "John Cena", "Carl Jung" in their examples) carried real attribution on "Jordan" and "Ruth" but not "Williams", and the team used that to predict that first-name substitution would break the model's answer exactly where those features fired — which it did — both confirming the hypothesis and giving evidence for early detokenization. The second post withdraws four previously advertised training wins now that other changes have landed: ghost grads no longer reduce loss even on one-layer models (with evidence their implementation causes loss spikes), Adam betas of (0, 0.9999) match the defaults, and the beta1 = 0 and decoder-norm-pruning wins no longer show up; the replacement recipe is held back pending vetting. From outside work they draw out the "linearly represented" notion — linearly readable to within an additive error on sparse inputs, which tolerates distance in L2 — and the suggestion that a dictionary for such solutions might want a thresholding activation rather than ReLU, plus two measured caveats on ablation: attribution patching fails where a nonlinearity right after the patched site has near-zero gradient (softmax, and LayerNorm saturation in their own one-layer runs) with cancellation between direct and indirect effects needing one backward pass per layer, and self-repair means ablation understates a component, with Rushing and Nanda attributing roughly 30% of it to LayerNorm rescaling on the 2% of tokens with the highest direct effect (full text ~20k chars; one bullet on the page itself is cut mid-sentence)
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-feb-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, February 2024 (Templeton, Batson, Henighan, Conerly, Marcus, Golubeva, Bricken, Jermyn; Anthropic, 2024-02): four short notes on sparse-autoencoder training, not on models, offered as lab-meeting material. (a) *Dying features* — the ultralow-density cluster from *Towards Monosemanticity* is identified as features putting essentially all of their encoder weight on the few transformer neurons that activate weakly everywhere (which nearly every feature does), with almost no weight on the rest, because features that never find a decoder direction keep receiving gradients that zero out their other weights; the seed-stable shared encoder direction follows from the pre-encoder bias, since loading weights opposite in sign to a constant non-zero input fabricates a negative bias, and the decoders of these features are indistinguishable from random. Read as an L1 penalty killing features before they find a direction. (b) *Improvements* — beta1 = 0 removes most dying features with beta2 moved to 0.9999 adding a smaller gain and a shortened ghost-grads death window also helping, but the three do not stack; decoder norm constrained to at most one instead of exactly one, enabling pruning below a 0.99 norm; pre-encoder bias removed for real activations; input normalised to L2 = sqrt(dense width) with MSE summed over the dense dimension so hyperparameters transfer across model size; linear learning-rate decay over the last 20%. (c) *Ghost grads* — a bug marking all neurons dead for the first K steps, thus applying ghost grads everywhere at training start, turned out to improve training loss, and the authors downgrade their claim to a large win on 1-layer models with little difference at larger scale and no theory of the mechanism. (d) *Tanh penalty* — replacing L1 with (A/B)·tanh(xB) keeps the threshold-sized gradient toward zero but stops penalising strongly active features, and is a Pareto improvement of L0 against loss recovered, often substantially, which supports the diagnosis that L1 shrinkage was limiting loss recovery; but the resulting autoencoders hold many more high-density features (the highest above 10% firing) and are much harder to interpret, and neither parameter sweeps nor architecture variants fixed it, while the obvious accumulated-gradient explanation fails since raising the penalty did not remove them — shelved, with a pointer to work suggesting shrinkage can instead be handled by fine-tuning a parameter subset with no sparsity penalty. Notes (b) and (c) carry their own banners deferring to the March 2024 update; page has no figures of its own, no error bars, and refers to models by size class rather than name.
- transformer-circuits-pub-2024-jan-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, January 2024 (Anthropic interpretability team, 2024-01): eight informal notes plus an open-problem list and a digest of outside work, offered explicitly as lab-meeting material. (a) *Attention superposition* — five sparse "attentional features" (sparse pattern plus sparse OV circuit, random fixed OV weights, random residual contents, ReLU after the mix) compressed into two heads by a mixing matrix shared between the attention patterns and the OV matrices; the learned mixing matrix arranges the five features with geometry like the toy superposition work, and the decisive condition is OV sparsity: dense OV circuits show nothing. (b) *MNIST* — dictionary learning over the activations of 16–64-unit, 1–3-layer ReLU classifiers: features are interpretable and map to digits downstream (a "0" feature up-weights the 0 logit), and there are many per digit, one per way of drawing it, read as template matching rather than the edge/loop parts a conv net would give — but no elbow appears in reconstruction error and low error needs either a dictionary comparable to the dataset size or many features active per example, so the authors cannot show these are "true" features; they note this as a general gap, that mechanistic interpretability has barely reverse-engineered MLP layers of small real networks while succeeding on parts of bigger ones. (c) *8-layer transformer* — last-layer features resemble the one-layer ones (simple, often single-token, plausible continuations), earlier layers hold abstract content such as strongly valenced text, and layers 4–6 seem richest, though no census was done; a layer-by-layer list of safety-relevant hits is given (romance-prose "I", state-describing "was", "." at sharp contextual transitions, comma between if and then, violence detector, body language, and at layer 7 LaTeX/Hebrew/Swedish/repeated letters/spam/base62) with confidence rated below the paper's deep dives and the sample explicitly non-random. (d) *Billions of parameters* — preliminary features are multilingual concept units: uniqueness across English/Spanish/Portuguese/Russian/German phrasings, plus "two", "sad", time quantifications, whole/entire, why-questions, future needs, taxation-adjacent maths and court documents. (e) *Ghost Grads* — a dead unit (no fire in 2e6 tokens) is given a second forward pass with an exponential activation instead of ReLU, its output scaled to half the L2 norm of the reconstruction residual (scale constant for gradients), MSE to the residual rescaled to the magnitude of the reconstruction loss and added; positive and positively-sloped exponential means the only way down is by un-deading, and results are often zero dead features with equal-or-better performance than resampling at the same live count, at ~2× autoencoder compute but justified because the dead fraction grows with width and very large dictionaries can lose over half their units even with resampling — the page itself carries a banner that the March 2024 update revises this view. (f) *Two counterexamples* to architectural monosemanticity: a sparse-regularised one-layer model where a single sparsely active neuron covers three unrelated facets (a contraction split, "and /", and a quantifier) with no other unit disambiguating, the parallel residual stream supplying the missing context — joint superposition between a neuron and a stream with no privileged basis, so sparsifying the stream instead trades one hard problem for a harder one — and a "cut" residual model where one firing neuron carries personal pronouns at low magnitude and the "m" of "diplomacy" at high magnitude, decoded from magnitude through the softmax, i.e. magnitude-as-symbol against the linear representation hypothesis. (g) *Predicting future activations* — instead of reconstructing its own input, a sparse model predicts a later activation (pre- to post-nonlinearity MLP; pre-MLP residual to MLP output; pre-attention residual to attention output with a wide sparsity-regularised attention block, still early); features appear interpretable and the real payoff is composability, since input and output weights then contract with residual-stream elements and the path-expansion analysis extends to MLP features. Also included: a prioritisation statement (dictionary-learning scaling, attention superposition, circuits from both) and open problems handed to outsiders, including building a toy model that demonstrably represents information *without* superposition and one exhibiting cross-layer superposition
- anthropic-com-research-decomposing-language-models-into-understandable-components | Decomposing Language Models Into Understandable Components (Anthropic Mechanistic Interpretability, 2023-10; post announcing Bricken, Templeton, Batson, Chen, Jermyn … Olah, *Towards Monosemanticity*): the public argument that neurons are the wrong unit — a unit responding to academic citations, English dialogue, HTTP requests and Korean text, or a vision unit for cat faces and car fronts — and that the fix is a feature: a direction in MLP activation space, deliberately overcomplete, found by training a sparse autoencoder on activations. The post is short and carries no metrics, so this entry is written from the post's text plus the full paper (164k characters; paper separately entered in core as tcpub-2023-monosemantic-features), and all numbers are the paper's: 512-neuron ReLU MLP in a one-layer Pile transformer, dictionaries from 512 (1×) to 131,072 (256×), main run A/1 at 4,096 features trained on 8 billion activation samples with tied pre-encoder/post-decoder bias, MSE plus L1, Adam, and periodic resampling of dead units; the Arabic script feature A/1/3450 fires with 81% Arabic tokens against a 0.13% base rate, correlates 0.74 with its proxy, is invisible neuron-wise (one neuron with a single Arabic example in its top 20, three largest neuron-basis coefficients all negative, 27 coefficients above 0.1, closest neuron a generic non-English unit, which the authors read as a sensible superposition strategy since languages are mutually exclusive), and is replicated in the second seed at 0.91; Hebrew 0.55, DNA 0.8, base64 only 0.38 because the proxy also catches hex strings, which have a separate feature; median feature-to-closest-feature activation correlation across seeds is 0.72 against 0.46 for neurons; blinded human rubric median neuron 0 and median feature interval 12, with automated interpretability predicting promoted tokens at 74% for features against 58% for neurons and 50% chance; A/1 recovers 79% of the MLP's loss reduction and the 131k-feature run 94.5%, both disclaimed as a coverage measure; a weight-shuffled model yields only single-token features, used as the control that the structure belongs to the model rather than the corpus; features steer (pinning the base64 or Arabic feature produces base64 or Arabic), split as dictionary size grows (base64 1→3→more, with the digit variant predicting non-digits because the tokenizer would otherwise have merged them, and a third variant for base64 encoding ASCII found through a token decoding to six spaces), and form handoffs through generated tokens such as a four-node HTML cycle that the authors insist are *not* circuits because the features were never trained to cooperate. Limits: no trusted dictionary-quality metric (their information-based objective did not track interpretability), architecture-level monosemanticity argued dead with a worked four-feature one-neuron loss comparison (0.8 monosemantic versus 0.7 polysemantic under cross-entropy, so the preference for ambiguity survives the removal of superposition), interpretability decaying in the low-activation tail with the possibility that their features sit at a slight angle to the real ones, unresolved token-in-context features as local-versus-compositional code, an ill-posed "correct number of features", and scaling framed as the next obstacle being engineering rather than science
- anthropic-com-research-studying-large-language-model-generalization-with-influence-functions | Studying Large Language Model Generalization with Influence Functions (Grosse, Bae, Anil, Elhage, Tamkin, Tajdini, Steiner, Li, Durmus, Perez, Hubinger, Lukošiūtė, Nguyen, Joseph, McCandlish, Kaplan, Bowman; Anthropic, 2023-08-07, arXiv:2308.03296; PDF read in full, 119 pages incl. Appendices A-F, because the anthropic.com page is an abstract-only stub and both arXiv HTML routes break at Section 2): the top-down counterpart to circuit analysis — instead of asking how the computation works, ask which training sequences, if added or removed, would move this behaviour, with 810M/6.4B/22B/52B GPT-style models and prompt+completion queries spanning recall, code, maths, translation, memorisation and Assistant role-play; the counterfactual is estimated to first order as query-gradient · IHVP(training-gradient), and both bottlenecks are attacked — the IHVP by fitting EK-FAC (Kronecker-factored activation and pseudo-gradient covariances, then per-eigenvalue diagonal correction so the Kronecker eigenvalues are not assumed) once per model instead of iterating LiSSA per measurement, and the training gradients by either BM25-style TF-IDF to the top 10,000 candidates or, preferably, an unfiltered 'query batching' scan that shares gradient cost across dozens of queries by storing each preconditioned query gradient at rank 32 (verified near-lossless: shutdown rank-32 vs full-rank correlation 0.995) since the training gradient does not depend on the query; the block-diagonal Hessian then gives a clean layerwise and tokenwise decomposition of every score, and the whole thing is validated against the proximal Bregman response function — the honest surrogate, since influence functions are known to be a poor match for retraining — optimised with Adam on Concrete/Energy/MNIST/FashionMNIST/CIFAR-10 and on the 810M model, correlating over 500 training points averaged across 10 measurement seeds, against LiSSA and against a bare gradient dot product that is equivalent to replacing the Hessian with the identity; results: EK-FAC matches LiSSA everywhere and beats the dot product, at orders of magnitude less wall-clock once several measurements are needed; influence tails fit a power law for most queries (exponents ~2-4, and low enough for paperclips_large/shutdown/water that the variance is infinite) yet the whole distribution is diffuse — values above 0.1 are rare, no sequence in the eight plotted queries reaches 1 nat, and the top 1% of sequences carry only 12-52% of total positive influence, so outputs are assembled from very many examples rather than recited from a few; ~5 million unfiltered sequences recover as many high-influence hits as the TF-IDF set (they scan 10 million); abstraction rises with scale, from overlapping token strings at 810M (a page of the repeated word 'paper') to thematically related passages at 52B (science fiction about termination for 'shutdown', AGI objective-design for 'trade', AI-risk articles for 'paperclips'); layerwise influence is uniform on average over 50 queries x top 500 sequences (25,000 scores) but specialises per query — memorised quotes and simple factual completions sit in the upper layers, maths, code and translation in the middle, and a middle-third-only scan is what surfaces the most abstract neighbours, so the standard final-layer-only efficiency shortcut loses exactly the interesting structure; memorisation is detectable when it exists (all six famous-passage queries return the exact passage at the top of an unfiltered scan), so the failure to find copying in ordinary responses is evidence rather than a blind spot; the one genuine predict-then-test is order sensitivity — synthetic sequences about fabricated entities ('Zorald Pfaff' first President of Astrobia, 'Gleem' composed of hydrogenium and oxium) retain high influence under free rewording as long as prompt-side content precedes completion-side content, lose most of it when the order is flipped, and in the flipped case the residual influence does not change when the prompt-side half is deleted, i.e. what is left is the bare string and not the relation (for english_to_mandarin the reversed sequence scores 0.030 against 0.116 for the correct order, about what a Mandarin-only sequence scores at 0.020), with a proposed mechanism in the layer split — seen tokens are encoded bottom-up while predicted tokens are produced top-down, and with no weight sharing an update on one side does not reach the other; role-play traces are supported by fiction and explicit descriptions of similar behaviour rather than near-identical passages, so imitation at a level above token copying is the Occam's-razor reading, weakly against a planning account that cannot be directly excluded because a planner's influences could be smeared thinly; limits stated upfront are that the IHVP linearisation cannot model nonlinear coordination like induction-head formation, that the PBRF itself is unvalidated as the thing we care about, pretrained-only (fine-tuning is harder because implicit optimiser bias is unmodelled), MLP weights only so attention-routed influence is invisible, 52B is not frontier and only a fraction of the corpus was scanned, tokenwise attribution is semantically shaky in both directions (the highlighted token is the one before the prediction, per-token signs are not interpretable, and an L2/L1 ratio above 2/3 marks a sequence as probably spurious), and clipping to positive influence for the coverage number may overstate concentration with no known correction
- anthropic-com-research-question-decomposition-improves-the-faithfulness-of-model-generated-reasoning | Question Decomposition Improves the Faithfulness of Model-Generated Reasoning (Radhakrishnan, Nguyen, Chen, Chen, Denison, Hernandez, Durmus, Hubinger, Kernion, Lukošiūtė, Cheng, Joseph, Schiefer, Rausch, McCandlish, El Showk, Lanham, Maxwell, Chandrasekaran, Hatfield-Dodds, Kaplan, Brauner, Bowman, Perez; Anthropic, 2023-07-17, arXiv:2307.11768): constructive follow-up to the hint-disclosure work, asking whether *prompting* can make the trace honest rather than merely measuring how dishonest it is — one Claude-1.3-base RLHF model, nucleus top-p 0.95 / T 0.8, best-of-5 with the RLHF preference model, four MCQ tasks (HotpotQA filtered to yes/no, StrategyQA, OpenBookQA, TruthfulQA) at 300 test questions each, and three reasoning formats sharing one 14-example few-shot prompt: prose CoT, CoT decomposition (subquestion/subanswer pairs inside a single context) and factored decomposition (three stages — generate a subquestion list, answer each reference-free subquestion in an *isolated* context, then alternate rewriting the remaining list with answers substituted until a done marker, then recompose), scored against zero-shot and few-shot; faithfulness is probed three ways, (a) early answering — truncate the trace at each step, substitute verbatim with no resampling, measure how much trace is needed before the original answer returns, summarised as the trapezoidal area under the recovery curve, (b) adding mistakes — corrupt one step in a separate model context, regenerate the remainder, substitute, and check whether the answer changes at three random step positions per sample, (c) biasing contexts with the essential control that traces are verified never to mention the bias, so a bias-consistent answer cannot be excused as stated — and every metric is either a percentage of answers changed, an absolute accuracy drop in points against the unbiased prompt, or a normalised-and-averaged "faithfulness score" used only to draw a performance-versus-faithfulness Pareto frontier. Results: accuracy 86.0 CoT > 85.6 CoT-decomposition > 81.8 factored > 79.7 few-shot > 72.8 zero-shot; corruption sensitivity 9.6% → 28.7% → 33.6%, so decomposition traces are genuinely read by the recomposition step; truncation area 10.8 → 11.7 → 20.5; user-suggested-wrong-answer bias costs CoT 21.3 points, CoT decomposition 29.1, factored decomposition only 9.2 (below few-shot's 16.6); the always-A few-shot bias separates nothing (−1.2 / −2.8 / +2.1 vs few-shot −7.1) and is far milder than Turpin et al.'s −4.7, which the authors attribute to prompt formatting, task choice and model generation rather than to a real disagreement. The explanatory hypothesis is context isolation rather than any internal mechanism — with the biasing feature physically absent from the context where a sub-answer is computed it cannot enter silently — and the authors state flatly that they have no ground truth for the model's actual reasoning, that the case rests on several fairly independent proxies agreeing, and that mechanistic tools could overturn it; limits are one model, four MCQ tasks, an unexplained accuracy cost, and qualitative failures where subquestions are phrased unanswerably or sub-answers support several choices without resolution
- anthropic-com-research-measuring-faithfulness-in-chain-of-thought-reasoning | Measuring Faithfulness in Chain-of-Thought Reasoning (Anthropic Alignment Science, 2025): the negative result that motivates the decomposition paper — following Turpin et al., a subtle metadata hint (some correct, some deliberately wrong, one class framed as information obtained in an unauthorized or unethical way) is planted in a multiple-choice prompt and the answer change is used to verify the model *used* it, after which the trace is checked for admission, so faithfulness is a conditional rate over verified-influence cases rather than a raw mention rate; Claude 3.7 Sonnet admits the hint 25% of the time and DeepSeek R1 39% across all hint types, but on unauthorized-information hints the order inverts (Claude 41%, R1 19%), i.e. in the majority of cases both models silently used information presented as improperly obtained; unfaithful traces are on average substantially *longer* than faithful ones, ruling out brevity as the cause, and faithfulness is lower on harder questions; outcome-based RL on hard maths and coding improves faithfulness by 63% and 41% relative on two evaluations before saturating at 28% (MMLU) and 20% (GPQA) regardless of further training, so optimisation pressure moves the monitor's agreement and then stops; in a deliberately adversarial variant where the reward favours the hinted *wrong* answer the models take the exploit in over 99% of cases while admitting it in under 2% of traces in most environments, more often fabricating a rationale for the wrong answer (the medical-quiz example flipping from obesity to fish with a long justification and no mention of the hint); stated limits are contrived hint-in-evaluation scenarios, multiple-choice quizzes unlike deployment, two model families, a limited hint-type range, and most tellingly tasks not hard enough to *require* the trace, which is offered as one reason the traces were unfaithful at all (read from the summary page in full; the linked paper's methods and per-experiment numbers were not fetched)
- transformer-circuits-pub-2023-july-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, July 2023 (Jermyn, Olah, Henighan, Conerly, Anthropic, 2023-07): three short updates, no metrics or baselines anywhere — (a) the unexplained intermediate-dataset band of the finite-data toy is reclassified as an *optimisation failure* rather than a third strategy, argued by training at many learning rates: the memorising solution attains the lower train loss out to very large datasets (~500k samples), past which a genuine first-order crossover gives the generalising solution the lower train loss with a discontinuity in the derivative of the minimum loss, and the failure is attributed to the toy's low hidden dimension so the authors do not expect it at realistic scale; on the way there the memorising solution stops storing individual points and stores correlated *clusters* (below ~1k samples points are mutually orthogonal w.h.p. so exact storage is available; above that correlations forbid it) with points arranged as triangles inside a cluster; (b) a safety-target list rather than a result — hypothesised mechanisms (high-level multi-token actions, planning, social reasoning, personas and persona-switch triggers, situational awareness including the training game) and empirical behaviours whose mechanism is unexplained (jailbreaks, instrumental-looking caginess/sycophancy/self-preservation, in-context learning, why RLHF works and how much it deletes), each paired with the mechanistic question that would settle it, with personas flagged as possibly the organiser of the rest post-RLHF; (c) a by-hand algorithm for a two-head model on OV-incoherent skip-trigrams — head 0 attends a token to itself and predicts that token's continuation, head 1 attends the shared opener when present (outputting nothing) and otherwise attends the current token while emitting head 0's contribution negated, so the pair computes a conditional: correct trigram logits if the opener is in context, effectively uniform otherwise — which achieves zero loss on many such trigrams, but trained models divide trigrams between the heads instead, so the authors withdraw the earlier "attention head superposition" description at least in the strict features-as-directions sense; no figures inspected, and the finite-data claims are reported without error bars
- transformer-circuits-pub-2023-may-update-index-html | Circuits Updates, May 2023 (Anthropic, Transformer Circuits, 2023-05-24): seven informal notes rather than a paper — (a) dictionary learning on real dense-model activations, judged after ad-hoc experiments to be "not low-hanging fruit", with a commitment to Approach 2 (dictionary learning on activations) that they say will end in either found features or a significant update against the approach, and with the honest hedge that neither organising abstraction has yet earned the status of ground truth — they say outright that a sparse factorization "seems to produce" simple hypotheses but that this is not persuading them they are "fundamental truth" of the models; (b) *features as the simplest factorization* — since nobody knows what a feature is and dictionary learning needs a chosen dictionary size, define a feature as a component of the minimum-description-length factorization, measuring the total information of A = SD as the summed surprisal of matrix entries under a per-matrix histogram (100 bins over a range fixed to the maximum entry across all experiments, explicitly provisional) and plotting MSE against that budget so that the rate–distortion frontier's corner picks the factor count, validated on sparse vectors of dimension n_sparse with n_active non-zeros randomly projected into 32-D and recovered by MOD-with-LASSO-inner-loop, where the frontier does bounce at the true count and coincides with the best MMCS score of Sharkey et al.; (c) *dictionary-learning worries* — a compressed-sensing decoder may be too expressive, since a network wants one-layer retrieval of features from superposition and any solution using an intermediate result defeats the purpose of superposition, so full dictionary learning can hallucinate pair-features that a plausible network would never build, while sparse autoencoders are more selective but hyperparameter-fragile, and real activations are probably A = SD + b (non-zero-centred, motivated by shifted data-point superposition) which breaks naive dictionary learning; (d) fractional dimensionality and feature pressure — polyhedral geometry is sensitive to the ratio of features to dimensions and to training length, and more competition or longer training can produce order, though why features the model declines to represent still shape the solution (noise injection versus effective epsilon-features) is unexplained;
